Loss of Sleep Need: Not feeling the urge to sleep or the thought that sleep is necessary.\n  - Indifference to Insomnia: Feeling no problem and continuing activities despite staying up for several nights.\n  - Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli: Attention is immediately drawn to trivial and unimportant stimuli nearby (e.g., noise, movement).\n  - Frequent Task Switching: Unable to finish one task and constantly shifting attention to new things, starting multiple tasks simultaneously.\n  - Inability to Sustain Attention: Unable to focus on any activity for long, with attention constantly being scattered.\n  Duration: 5 months\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Social Withdrawal: Reduced interest or effort in social interactions and maintaining relationships.\n  - Loss of Motivation: Lack of motivation to initiate or complete work or tasks.\n  - Anhedonia: Inability to experience positive emotions such as happiness, satisfaction, love, or pleasure.\n  - Indifference to Achievement: Insensitivity or indifference to personal achievements or success.\n  - Initial Insomnia: Difficulty falling asleep and taking a long time to do so.\n  - Middle Insomnia: Waking up frequently during sleep or having difficulty falling back asleep.\n  - Early Morning Awakening: Waking up too early and being unable to fall back asleep.\n  - Hypersomnia: Sleeping for long periods at night or excessive sleep during the day.\n  - Nightmare Disorder: Sleep disturbance caused by repeated and distressing dreams.\n  - Elevated Mood: A mood that is abnormally high, euphoric, and full of unmotivated happiness and vitality.\n  - Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Hyperactivity: Doing far more work than usual or starting multiple activities simultaneously.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Inability to tolerate resting or stopping, driven by a constant need to do something.\n  - Reduced Sleep Duration: Sleeping significantly less than usual but feeling refreshed rather than tired.\n  - Loss of Sleep Need: Not feeling the urge to sleep or the thought that sleep is necessary.\n  - Indifference to Insomnia: Feeling no problem and continuing activities despite staying up for several nights.\n  - Talkativeness: Speaking far more than usual and continuing to talk without rest.\n  - Rapid Speech: Speech speed is so fast that it is difficult for others to understand or interrupt.\n  - Pressured Speech: Cannot stop talking or tolerate silence due to an internal pressure to keep speaking.\n  - Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli: Attention is immediately drawn to trivial and unimportant stimuli nearby (e.g., noise, movement).\n  - Frequent Task Switching: Unable to finish one task and constantly shifting attention to new things, starting multiple tasks simultaneously.\n  - Inability to Sustain Attention: Unable to focus on any activity for long, with attention constantly being scattered.\n  Duration: 5 months\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Social Withdrawal: Reduced interest or effort in social interactions and maintaining relationships.\n  - Loss of Motivation: Lack of motivation to initiate or complete work or tasks.\n  - Anhedonia: Inability to experience positive emotions such as happiness, satisfaction, love, or pleasure.\n  - Indifference to Achievement: Insensitivity or indifference to personal achievements or success.\n  - Initial Insomnia: Difficulty falling asleep and taking a long time to do so.\n  - Middle Insomnia: Waking up frequently during sleep or having difficulty falling back asleep.\n  - Early Morning Awakening: Waking up too early and being unable to fall back asleep.\n  - Hypersomnia: Sleeping for long periods at night or excessive sleep during the day.\n  - Nightmare Disorder: Sleep disturbance caused by repeated and distressing dreams.\n  - Non-Restorative Sleep: Feeling unrefreshed despite having slept enough.\n  Duration: 1 year and 1 months\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list related\n  sections/items in matched_sections, and write a short English strategy in answer_strategy\n  on what tone and content the patient should reply with.\n- If the question is not in the profile or can only be inferred, set matched=false and instruct\n  in answer_strategy to respond briefly (e.g., \"I'm not sure\", \"I haven't thought about it\").\n- If patient_turn_index is 1 (patient's first reply), ONLY focus the strategy on the chief complaint\n  (do NOT reveal other symptoms).\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"matched\": true|false, \"matched_sections\": [\"...\"], \"answer_strategy\": \"...\"}\n"

Elevated Mood: A mood that is abnormally high, euphoric, and full of unmotivated happiness and vitality.\n  - Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Hyperactivity: Doing far more work than usual or starting multiple activities simultaneously.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Inability to tolerate resting or stopping, driven by a constant need to do something.\n  - Reduced Sleep Duration: Sleeping significantly less than usual but feeling refreshed rather than tired.\n  - Loss of Sleep Need: Not feeling the urge to sleep or the thought that sleep is necessary.\n  - Indifference to Insomnia: Feeling no problem and continuing activities despite staying up for several nights.\n  - Talkativeness: Speaking far more than usual and continuing to talk without rest.\n  - Rapid Speech: Speech speed is so fast that it is difficult for others to understand or interrupt.\n  - Pressured Speech: Cannot stop talking or tolerate silence due to an internal pressure to keep speaking.\n  - Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli: Attention is immediately drawn to trivial and unimportant stimuli nearby (e.g., noise, movement).\n  - Frequent Task Switching: Unable to finish one task and constantly shifting attention to new things, starting multiple tasks simultaneously.\n  - Inability to Sustain Attention: Unable to focus on any activity for long, with attention constantly being scattered.\n  Duration: 5 months\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Social Withdrawal: Reduced interest or effort in social interactions and maintaining relationships.\n  - Loss of Motivation: Lack of motivation to initiate or complete work or tasks.\n  - Anhedonia: Inability to experience positive emotions such as happiness, satisfaction, love, or pleasure.\n  - Indifference to Achievement: Insensitivity or indifference to personal achievements or success.\n  - Initial Insomnia: Difficulty falling asleep and taking a long time to do so.\n  - Middle Insomnia: Waking up frequently during sleep or having difficulty falling back asleep.\n  - Early Morning Awakening: Waking up too early and being unable to fall back asleep.\n  - Hypersomnia: Sleeping for long periods at night or excessive sleep during the day.\n  - Nightmare Disorder: Sleep disturbance caused by repeated and distressing dreams.\n  - Non-Restorative Sleep: Feeling unrefreshed despite having slept enough.\n  Duration: 1 year and 1 months\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: hypomanic_episode\nAnswer strategy: Confirm that during those five months, your elevated symptoms did not lead to hospitalization, cause you to lose touch with reality (psychosis), or completely disable your ability to function, even though you were highly energetic, talkative, and active."

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I'm visiting today because I've been struggling with a severe lack of interest, low motivation, and feeling down for over a year. Along with that, I've been experiencing intense fluctuations in my mood and energy, sometimes feeling extremely elevated and irritable. It has just become too difficult to handle on my own.\n\nDoctor: When you have those elevated or irritable periods, do they last at least 4 days or even a week, and during them do you need much less sleep or do risky or unusual things?\n\nPatient: Yes, those high-energy periods actually last much longer than a week—they've been going on for about five months now. During these times, I sleep significantly less but feel completely refreshed and don't feel the need for it. I don't really do dangerous things, but I become extremely hyperactive, talkative, and constantly switch between tasks.\n\nDoctor: During those five months, did these symptoms cause major problems like being unable to work or needing hospitalization, or did you ever have hallucinations or strongly fixed beliefs that others said were not true?\n\nPatient: No, I never experienced any hallucinations or strongly fixed beliefs, and I didn't need to be hospitalized. Even though it was intense, I was still able to manage my daily life and keep working during those five months.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have you felt depressed most days more often than not, with symptoms never going away for more than two months at a time?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it has been a full two years. But I have been struggling with these depressive feelings consistently for a bit over a year now, about a year and a month.\n\nDoctor: During those five months of elevated mood, did your symptoms clearly cause a marked change from your usual self that others noticed or that significantly strained your relationships, even if you kept working?\n\nPatient: Yes, there was definitely a highly noticeable change, and people around me absolutely pointed out that I was acting differently. They were overwhelmed because I was talking incredibly fast, constantly switching tasks, and barely sleeping without ever feeling tired. My relationships were also strained because my mood would swing so rapidly from being extremely euphoric to suddenly very irritable.\n\nDoctor: Even though you kept working, during that five-month period did your symptoms ever become so severe that you were unable to function safely or appropriately in important situations, like at work, socially, or financially?\n\nPatient: No, it never got to the point where I couldn't function safely or handle my responsibilities. I was extremely hyperactive and definitely difficult for others to be around, but I still managed to keep things under control at work and in my daily life.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had a separate period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day, with symptoms like loss of interest, low energy, sleep or appetite changes, guilt, or trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes, I had a separate period of depression that lasted for about a year and a month. During that time, I completely lost interest in my hobbies, withdrew from friends, and had absolutely no motivation. I also had terrible sleep issues, where I'd struggle to fall asleep, wake up too early, or sometimes sleep too much but still feel completely unrefreshed.\n\nDoctor: During that five-month elevated period, did your symptoms ever clearly require hospitalization, include psychosis, or cause marked impairment so severe that you could not keep up normal functioning?"

No, I never needed to be hospitalized, and I didn't experience any psychosis or lose touch with reality. Even though I was extremely energetic, talkative, and constantly active, I was still able to keep up my normal functioning and manage my daily life.

Even though I was extremely energetic, talkative, and constantly active, I was still able to keep up my normal functioning and manage my daily life.\n\nDoctor: During those elevated periods, were these changes clearly different from your usual self and noticeable to others, but without ever becoming severe enough to cause hospitalization, psychosis, or inability to function?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.
